).

9:

Compute
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{(k)}
using (
5
).

10:

end

for

11:

Solve
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*}\equiv\mathrm{argmin}_{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}L\big\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{g}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}^{(k)},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{(k)};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}})\big\}
to obtain the masking NBE,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{g}$}}}(\cdot,\cdot;{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*})
.

12:

Estimation stage
(fast, repeatable for arbitrarily many observed data sets)

1:

Observed data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
c\in\mathbb{R}
for use in (
4
).

2:

Compute
\textstyle{U}
using (
4
).

3:

Compute
\textstyle{W}
using (
5
).

4:

Return
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{g}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{g}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*})
.

Figure 1
:
The Estimation stage of Algorithm
1
. Observed data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
, and the associated indices
\mathcal{I}_{1}
(here, implicit) that identify which elements of
\textstyle{Z}
are observed, are used to construct
\textstyle{U}
, a masked version of the complete data
\textstyle{Z}
with missing entries replaced by a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\textstyle{W}
, a vector of indicator variables that encode the missingness pattern. The encoded data
\textstyle{U}
and
\textstyle{W}
are then input to an NBE to obtain point estimates
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}
of a model parameter
\textstyle{\theta}
.

Algorithm
1
has a number of strengths. It only requires simulation from
p(\mathcal{I}_{1}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
and
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, and no information on
\textstyle{\theta}
is lost by using
\textstyle{U}
and
\textstyle{W}
instead of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, since there is a one-to-one mapping between these quantities (see Theorem
1
in Section
S1
of the Supplementary Material). However, the approach has two drawbacks. First, the neural network must learn a mapping from
\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{W}
to
\Theta
. This learning task can be more challenging than learning a mapping from
\mathcal{Z}
to
\Theta
, since an element in
\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{W}
is semi-discrete and of dimension
2n
. Second, the quality of the fit is subject to a judicious choice of the missingness model. To see this, rewrite the Bayes risk (
1
) in terms of an estimator
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
treating
\textstyle{U}
and
\textstyle{W}
as the data:

r(\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot,\cdot))\equiv\sum_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}}\int_{\Theta}\int_{\mathcal{U}}L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}))p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}.

(8)

It is a straightforward corollary of Theorem
1
in Section
S1
of the Supplementary Material and Bayesian sufficiency
(e.g.,
Cox and Hinkley,, 1974
, Ch. 2)
that a Bayes estimator that minimizes (
8
) also minimizes the Bayes risk defined in terms of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
. Further, under the assumption that
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is independent of
\textstyle{\theta}
, which is often reasonable in practice, a Bayes estimator that minimizes (
8
) is invariant to distributions on
\mathcal{I}_{1}
that are positive on the power set of
\{1,\dots,n\}
(see Theorem
2
in Section
S1
of the Supplementary Material). However, in practice, the empirical analogue of (
8
), given by the sum on the right-hand-side of (
7
), is subject to Monte Carlo error that depends on the chosen distribution for
\mathcal{I}_{1}
. Therefore, the choice of distribution has practical implications on the approximation of the Bayes estimator obtained by substituting (
7
) into (
6
), particularly in high-dimensional settings where the total number of possible missingness patterns (
2^{n}
) is large and the missingness mechanism is difficult to specify. In Section
3
, we show through simulation that selecting a distribution for
\mathcal{I}_{1}
that assigns low probability to the observed missingness pattern can lead to statistically inefficient and biased estimators.

To address these limitations, in this paper we propose an alternative statistical approach to neural Bayes estimation with incomplete data. Our approach does not require the missingness pattern to be an input to the neural network, or the specification of a missingness mechanism. We do this by embedding neural networks in a classical data-augmentation approach to solving incomplete-data problems, namely an MCEM algorithm.

2.3
The EM approach for missing data

In Section
2.3.1
, we provide an overview of the classical EM algorithm and its Monte Carlo version. In Section
2.3.2
, we outline the general structure of our proposed EM approach to neural Bayes estimation with incomplete data. In Section
2.3.3
, we detail the construction of a neural approximation to the MAP estimator, which is needed for our EM approach.

2.3.1
The classical EM algorithm and its Monte Carlo version

Recall that we use
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}
to denote the subvectors of
\textstyle{Z}
that are treated as observed and missing, respectively, and that we use
\textstyle{Z}
to denote the complete data. We also have available an ordered set of indices,
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, associated with
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
. However, since one does not need to construct a mask from these indices in our approach described below, we omit the explicit notation of these indices in this subsection.

In the classical statistics literature, many algorithms have been developed based on the “data augmentation principle”, which is applied when inference based on
\textstyle{Z}
is easier than inference based only on
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}

(
Tanner and Wong,, 1987
;
van Dyk and Meng,, 2001
, see, e.g.,)
. A popular approach to point estimation that follows from this principle is the EM algorithm
(
Dempster et al.,, 1977
;
Wu,, 1983
;
McLachlan and Krishnan,, 2008
)
, an iterative algorithm for estimating
\textstyle{\theta}
with the
l
th iteration given by

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}=M(\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)})\equiv\argmax_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\in\Theta}\,\Big\{\log\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})+\mathbb{E}_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)}}\ell({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2})\Big\};\quad l=1,2,\dots,

(9)

where
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
denotes a prior density (sometimes referred to as a penalty function) and
\ell({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2})\equiv\log p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
denotes the complete-data log-likelihood.
The EM algorithm satisfies the ascent property, which means that the incomplete-data posterior density
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1})
is non-decreasing at each iteration. Thus, under mild conditions
(
Boyles,, 1983
;
Wu,, 1983
;
McLachlan and Krishnan,, 2008
, Ch. 3)
, it yields a local maximizer of

p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1})
.
For the special case of
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto 1
, the EM algorithm increases the incomplete-data likelihood
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
at each iteration and, therefore, yields a local maximizer of

p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
.

When the conditional expectation in (
9
) is intractable but conditional simulation is feasible, one often adopts a Monte Carlo version of the EM algorithm, MCEM
(
Wei and Tanner,, 1990
;
Ruth,, 2024
)
,
which has as the
l
th iteration,

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}=M_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(l-1)})\equiv\argmax_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\in\Theta}\bigg\{\log\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}^{(l,j)})\bigg\},

(10)

where
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}^{(l,j)}\}_{j=1}^{m}
are simulated from the conditional distribution of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)}
.
The frequentist MCEM algorithm is recovered as a special case of (
10
) by taking
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto 1
.

A practical challenge in implementing the MCEM algorithm is choosing the Monte Carlo sample size
m
. One way to tackle this challenge is through averaging
(
Fort and Moulines,, 2003
;
Cappé et al.,, 2005
, Sec. 11.1.2.2)
, where convergence assessment and final estimates are based on the averaged subsequence,

\bar{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}_{b}\equiv\frac{1}{l-b}\sum_{t=b+1}^{l}\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(t)},

(11)

where
b<l
denotes a burn-in chosen such that
\{\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(t)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}t=b+1,\dots,l\}
is approximately stationary. This averaging greatly reduces sensitivity to the choice of
m
, as Monte Carlo variability is effectively “averaged out” over multiple iterations. It is also formally motivated by the result of
Chan and Ledolter, (1995)
, that for large
m
, an MCEM sequence starting in a small neighborhood of a local maximizer of
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1})
can be approximated by a stationary first-order autoregressive (AR(1)) process centered at that maximizer. When employing averaging, the main consideration in choosing
m
is ensuring that the iterates become stable, in the sense that they eventually remain centered around a single point.
Since the Monte Carlo variability is driven by the conditional variance
\mathbb{V}_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}\{\ell({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2})\}
, which typically increases with both the tail-heaviness of the model and the proportion of missing data,
m
should be increased with both of these quantities whenever stability is a concern.

A drawback of the (MC)EM algorithm is that it can be slow since it is “doubly iterative” in the typical case where each maximization step requires numerical optimization. Although a substantial amount of work has been devoted to trying to speed up the algorithm, primarily by accelerating its rate of convergence
(
Louis,, 1982
;
Meng and Rubin,, 1993
;
Liu and Rubin,, 1994
;
Jamshidian and Jennrich,, 1997
;
Liu et al.,, 1998
;
Neal and Hinton,, 1998
;
Varadhan and Roland,, 2008
;
Lewandowski et al.,, 2010
, e.g.,)
, computational speed remains a practical limitation in many settings. Further, although the MCEM algorithm bypasses the conditional expectation in (
9
), it still requires evaluation of the complete-data log-likelihood function, which is not always possible. Next, we describe how the MCEM algorithm can incorporate NBEs with incomplete data in a manner that is not subject to these limitations.

2.3.2
NBEs and approximate MCEM algorithms

Our EM approach to using NBEs with incomplete data is predicated on the fact that (
10
) is equivalent to,

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}=M_{m}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)})=\argmax_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\in\Theta}\bigg\{\log\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}^{(l,j)})\bigg\},

(12)

where the probability density
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto\{\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\}^{m}
. The reformulation of (
10
) as (
12
) shows that the conventional MCEM update
M_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
is a MAP estimate under a modified prior distribution, which can be approximated by an NBE (denoted by
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}(\cdot)
in (
16
) and fitted according to (
17
) in Section
2.3.3
). This leads to our proposed EM approach (Algorithm
2
), a fast version of the MCEM algorithm that does not require the evaluation of any likelihood functions. Figure
2
illustrates the Estimation stage of the algorithm. After conditional simulation, the update (
12
) is obtained in a fraction of a second. Importantly, since the incomplete data are completed by conditional simulation, the NBE is applied to complete-data vectors only. Therefore, our proposed algorithm does not require a model for the missingness mechanism.

Algorithm 2

The EM approach to neural Bayes estimation with missing data.

1:

Training stage
(slow, to be done only once offline)

2:

Prior
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, number of training samples
K
, number of Monte Carlo samples
m
used in the Estimation stage, probability model
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, neural network
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}^{m}\to\Theta
parameterized by
\textstyle{\gamma}
.

3:

for

k=1,\dots,K

do

4:

Sample parameters
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)}\sim\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
.

5:

Simulate data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(k,j)}\sim p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)})
for
j=1,\dots,m
.

6:

end

for

7:

Solve
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*}\equiv\mathrm{argmin}_{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}\{\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)})\}^{m-1}L\big\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}(\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(k,j)}\}_{j=1}^{m};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}})\big\}
(see (
17
)) with
L(\cdot,\cdot)
a continuous and almost-everywhere differentiable approximation of the 0–1 loss function (e.g., (
15
)), to obtain the NBE,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}(\cdot\,;{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*})
, that approximates the MAP estimator.

8:

Estimation stage
(fast, repeatable for arbitrarily many observed data sets)

1:

Observed data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
, number of Monte Carlo samples
m
, initial estimates
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(0)}
, convergence criterion, burn-in
b
, maximum number of iterations, algorithm to simulate from the conditional distribution
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
.

2:

Set
l=0
.

3:

repeat

4:

Set
l=l+1
.

5:

Simulate missing data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}^{(l,j)}\sim p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)})
,
j=1,\dots,m
, resulting in
m
conditionally independent replicates of the completed data,
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(l,j)}\}_{j=1}^{m}
.

6:

Update
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}(\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(l,j)}\}_{j=1}^{m};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*})
.

7:

if

l>b

then

8:

Compute the post–burn-in mean
\bar{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}_{b}=\frac{1}{\,l-b\,}\sum_{t=b+1}^{l}\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(t)}
.

9:

Check convergence of
\bar{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}_{b}
according to the specified criterion.

10:

end

if

11:

until
converged or maximum number of iterations reached.

12:

Return
\bar{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}_{b}
.

Figure 2
:
The Estimation stage of Algorithm
2
. Incomplete data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
with missing entries are completed by conditional simulation using the previous parameter estimate
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)}
of model parameter
\textstyle{\theta}
. The
m
conditionally-independent replicates are then input to an NBE trained to approximate the MAP estimator. The parameter estimate
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}
is then used for conditional simulation in the next iteration of the algorithm.

Algorithm
2
is ideally suited when simulations can be carried out from the distributions of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}
, and when the incomplete-data and complete-data likelihoods are unavailable or computationally intractable. Statistical models that have these properties include (hidden) Markov random fields (e.g.,
Besag,
,
1974
;
Rue and Held,
,
2005
), such as the (hidden)
Potts, (1952)
model considered in Section
3.3
, the autologistic model
(
Besag,, 1972
)
, and other auto-models proposed by
Besag, (1974)
. These models have intractable likelihoods due to the computational complexity of the required normalizing constants. However, marginal and conditional simulation is feasible through Gibbs sampling.

Algorithm
2
is equivalent to the conventional MCEM algorithm, up to the error introduced by using an NBE to approximate the conventional update
M_{m}(\cdot)
. Indeed, it is one member of a broader family of approximate MCEM algorithms that may be expressed in the following general form:

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}=M_{m}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)})+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\delta}$}}}_{m}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l-1)}),\quad l=1,2,\dots,

(13)

where
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\delta}$}}}_{m}(\cdot)
denotes (random) approximation error. In Section
S2
of the Supplementary Material, we derive the asymptotic behavior of the sequence (
13
). For instance, for large
m
and with mean-zero approximation error, the sequence (
13
) behaves approximately as an inhomogoneous nonlinear AR(1) process centered on the EM update (
9
). Further, if
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{*}
is an isolated (local) maximizer of the incomplete data posterior density
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1})
, then a sequence (
13
) starting in the vicinity of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{*}
behaves approximately as a stationary AR(1) process with mean
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{*}
. These results provide a justification for Algorithm
2
and motivate its use with the averaging procedure in (
11
).

Finally, most of the considerations regarding the choice of Monte Carlo sample size
m
and the convergence criterion carry over directly from the conventional MCEM algorithm to Algorithm
2
. In particular, we employ the averaging prodedure (
11
) to improve robustness to the choice of
m
; and we adopt the convergence criterion of
Booth and Hobert, (1999)
that terminates the algorithm when the relative change in the averaged estimates falls below a specified tolerance
for a number of
successive iterations. Details on our specific choices for
m
, burn-in
b
, and convergence threshold are provided in Section
3.1
.

2.3.3
Neural MAP estimation

Algorithm
2
hinges on the capacity to approximate the MAP estimator using an NBE. Here, we outline how this can be
done,
and address additional practical considerations.

Approximating the MAP estimator.

For continuous parameter spaces, the MAP estimator is, under mild conditions
(
Bassett and Deride,, 2019
)
, the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
of the Bayes estimators associated with the 0–1 loss function

L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}})=\mathbb{I}(\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}-\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}\|>\epsilon),

(14)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes any norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

(
Robert,, 2007
, pg. 166)
, which in our work we choose to be the Euclidean norm.
However, (
14
) is not amenable to gradient-based methods for solving (
3
), and therefore it cannot be used to construct an NBE that approximates the MAP estimator. This challenge may be circumvented by noting that, under suitable regularity conditions, for large
m
and uniform prior
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, the objective in (
12
) tends to the logarithm of an unnormalized Gaussian density (Bernstein–von Mises theorem; see, e.g.,
van der Vaart,
,
1998
, pg. 140–141). Since the mean of a Gaussian random variable is also its mode, one could therefore choose a quadratic loss function instead of (
14
) under a uniform prior. For robustness reasons, we prefer to adopt a mathematically convenient surrogate for (
14
) with derivatives that are continuous almost everywhere; for example,

L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}};\kappa)\equiv\rm{tanh}(\|\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\|/\kappa),\quad\kappa>0,

(15)

where
\rm{tanh}(\cdot)
denotes the hyperbolic tangent function, yields the 0–1 loss function in the limit as
\kappa\to 0
(see
Section
S3
of the Supplementary Material,
where we also discuss alternative loss functions). Therefore, instead of (
14
) for some
\epsilon
close to 0, we use (
15
) for some
\kappa
close to 0.

Neural-network architecture for handling
m
conditionally independent replicates.

In Algorithm
2
, the update (
12
) involves neural Bayes estimation based on a set of
m
completed data vectors.
A suitable neural-network architecture for these data that ensures scalability with respect to
m
is DeepSets
(
Zaheer et al.,, 2017
)
:

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}(\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{m}\,;{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}})={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\phi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\phi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\phi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\phi}$}}}\Big\{\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\psi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\psi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\psi}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(j)};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}_{\psi});\,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}_{\phi}\Big\},

(16)

where
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{Z}
for
j=1,\dots,m
,

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\psi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\psi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\psi}$}}}(\cdot;{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}_{\psi})
is a neural network whose architecture depends on the structure of the data,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\phi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\phi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\phi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\phi}$}}}(\cdot;{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}_{\phi})
is an MLP, and the neural-network parameters are
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}=({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}_{\psi}^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}_{\phi}^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
. The representation (
16
) has several motivations.
First, when the data are exchangeable, the MAP estimator is invariant to permutations of the data, and estimators constructed from (
16
) are guaranteed to exhibit this property. Second, under certain conditions, (
16
) is a universal approximator for continuously differentiable permutation-invariant functions; therefore, any MAP estimator that is a continuously differentiable function of the data can be approximated arbitrarily well by an estimator of the form (
16
). Third, (
16
) may be used with any value of
m
.
Sainsbury-Dale et al., (2024)
give further details on the motivation and use of (
16
) in the general setting of neural Bayes estimation.

Accounting for the concentrated probability density
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto\{\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\}^{m}
.

The update (
12
) involves
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto\{\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\}^{m}
, the prior raised to the power of
m
. Note that the prior remains
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, since (
10
) and (
12
) are equivalent. There are at least two ways in which this distribution can be accounted for. In the first way, one may sample directly from
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
when constructing the set
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)}\}_{k=1}^{K}
, and then train the network as usual. In the second way, one may sample from
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
and adjust the objective function through an importance sampling scheme in which samples drawn from
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
are reweighted to approximate expectations under
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
. When constructing an NBE under the loss function (
15
), the optimization task becomes

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}^{*}\equiv\underset{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}\in\Gamma}{\mathrm{arg\,min}}\;\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\{\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)})\}^{m-1}L\big\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{h}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{h}$}}}(\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(k,j)}\}_{j=1}^{m};{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\gamma}$}}});\kappa\big\},

(17)

where, independently for each
k
,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)}\sim\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
and
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{(k,j)}\}_{j=1}^{m}
are simulated from
p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(k)})
.
The choice of how to account for
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
depends on whether it is easier to sample from
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
or to evaluate
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
. Since it is usually straightforward to evaluate
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, we present Algorithm
2
in terms of (
17
). Finally, when
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto 1
, the posterior is identical under
\pi_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
and
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, and no change to the workflow is required.

3
Simulation studies

We now conduct simulation studies to investigate the strengths and weaknesses of the masking approach (Algorithm
1
) compared to our EM approach (Algorithm
2
), both developed for estimation in the presence of missing data. We refer to an NBE employing the masking approach as a “Masking NBE”, and an NBE employing our EM approach as an “EM NBE”. In Section
3.1
, we outline the general setting. In Section
3.2
, we consider a spatial Gaussian-process model and estimate its parameters. In Section
3.3
, we examine the hidden
Potts, (1952)
model.

3.1
General setting

We conduct our experiments using functionality we have added to the package
NeuralEstimators

(
Sainsbury-Dale,, 2024
)
, which is available in
Julia
and
R
. We use a workstation with an AMD EPYC 7402 3.00GHz CPU with 128 GB of CPU RAM, and a Nvidia Quadro RTX 6000 GPU with 24 GB of GPU RAM. All subsequent results can be generated using reproducible code at
https://github.com/msainsburydale/NeuralIncompleteData
.

To elucidate the differences between the masking approach (Algorithm
1
) and our proposed EM approach (Algorithm
2
), which are greater with data that are high-dimensional, our simulation studies consider spatial models where the data are observed incompletely over a regular grid of size
n=64^{2}=4096
, and we therefore use a CNN-based architecture for (
6
) and (
16
) detailed in Section
S5
of the Supplementary Material. There, we also illustrate the benefits of using an ensemble

(
Hansen and Salamon,, 1990
)
of neural networks in the context of neural Bayes estimation;
throughout our experiments we use an ensemble of five NBEs for both the masking approach and the EM approach.

We train our NBEs under the pre-limiting 0–1 loss function (
15
), with
\kappa=0.1
and
K=25000
in both (
7
) (Masking) and (
17
) (EM). To avoid vanishing gradients early in training, we first pretrain each NBE using the mean-absolute-error loss function before switching to (
15
). During training, we use the default values of
NeuralEstimators
; specifically, we utilize the Adam optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba,, 2014
)
with an initial learning rate of 0.0005 and a cosine-annealing learning-rate schedule
(
Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2016
)
. We cease training when the objective function in (
7
) or (
17
) has not decreased in five consecutive epochs, where an epoch is defined to be one complete pass through the entire training data set when doing stochastic gradient descent to decrease the objective functions. When training the Masking NBEs, we use a missing-completely-at-random (MCAR) model for the missingness mechanism, with the percentage of missing data varying uniformly between 10% and 50% across data sets.
Figure
S5
of the Supplementary Material shows the value of the objective function in (
7
) or (
17
) evaluated at the end of each epoch.

Post training, the estimators’ statistical efficiencies are compared on unseen test data under both MCAR missingness and a model for the missingness where data are missing in a contiguous block (MICB). This is done to assess the Masking NBEs under the correct and an incorrect specification of the missingness mechanism.
We compute empirical root-mean-squared errors (RMSEs) based on simulated data using a new set of 1000 parameter vectors sampled from the prior. For the parameter vectors
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(1000)}\}
,

\text{RMSE}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot))=\bigg\{\frac{1}{1000}\sum_{j=1}^{1000}\|\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(j)}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(j)}\|^{2}\bigg\}^{1/2},

(18)

where, for
j=1,\dots,1000
,
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(j)}
is the corresponding estimate using the estimator
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot)
from incomplete data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{(j)}\sim p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(j)})
, and recall that
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Euclidean norm.
We use RMSE
{}_{\text{MCAR}}
and RMSE
{}_{\text{MICB}}
to denote the RMSE of an estimator based on incomplete data simulated under the MCAR and MICB mechanisms, respectively. For the EM NBEs, we set
m=30
in (
12
); we use the mean of the prior distribution for the initial estimates,
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(0)}
; we use a fixed burn-in of
b=5
in (
11
); and we stop the algorithm after 50 iterations or if the maximum elementwise relative change in
\bar{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{(l)}_{b}
, as defined in (
11
), is less than
0.001
for three consecutive values of
l
.

3.2
Gaussian process model

In this simulation study, we consider a spatial Gaussian process model, where
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\equiv(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
are data at locations
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{1},\dots,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{n}\}
in a spatial domain
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The data are modeled as spatially-correlated mean-zero Gaussian random variables with Matérn covariance function given by

{\rm cov\!}\left(Z_{i},\,Z_{j}\right)=\sigma^{2}\frac{2^{1-\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu)}\left(\frac{\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{i}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{j}\|}{\rho}\right)^{\nu}K_{\nu}\!\left(\frac{\|{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{i}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{j}\|}{\rho}\right)+\tau^{2}\mathbb{I}(i=j);\quad i,j=1,\dots,n,

(19)

where
\sigma^{2}
is a variance parameter;
\Gamma(\cdot)
is the gamma function;
K_{\nu}(\cdot)
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind of order
\nu
;
\rho>0
and
\nu>0
are range and smoothness parameters, respectively; and
\tau^{2}
is a fine-scale variance parameter.

In this example, we take the spatial domain to be
\mathcal{D}\equiv[0,1]\times[0,1]
, and we simulate complete data on a regular square grid of size
n=64^{2}=4096
. For computational tractability, we use the package
GpGp
to simulate training data via the
Vecchia, (1988)
approximation. We implement the Vecchia approximation using a “maxmin” ordering of the locations, with a maximum of 30 neighbors assigned to each location; see
Guinness, (2018)
for further details. The parameters to be estimated are
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\equiv(\tau,\rho)^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
, and we fix
\nu=1
and
\sigma^{2}=1
. We assume that
\tau
and
\rho
are independent
a priori
, and we use the priors
\tau\sim{\text{Unif}}(0.01,1)
and
\rho\sim{\text{Unif}}(0.03,0.35)
.

We compare the estimators using the time taken for their training and for estimation for a single data set post-training (computational efficiency); and their empirical RMSEs (statistical efficiency). Since the likelihood function is available for this model, we compare the two competing NBEs to the MAP estimator that numerically maximizes the incomplete-data posterior density.
Our results, summarized in Table
1
and Figure
3
, highlight several important properties of the estimators.

Table 1
:

training time,
estimation time for a single test data set, and empirical RMSE under two missingness models for
three
estimators of the parameters of the Gaussian process model (Section
3.2
).

Estimation time (s)

Figure 3
:
Spatial data (first column) where the missingness is of type MCAR (first row) or MICB (second row) with missingness shown in gray, and corresponding empirical distributions (second and third columns) for three estimators of the parameters of the Gaussian process model (Section
3.2
). True parameter values are shown as a dashed vertical line.

First, the EM NBE is agnostic to the missingness pattern and it performs well under both MCAR and MICB data, as illustrated by the similarity of its sampling distribution (Figure
3
) and RMSE (Table
1
) to those of the MAP estimator.
By contrast, the Masking NBE has substantial bias and inflated RMSE on MICB test data (recall that the Masking NBE was trained with MCAR data). The almost-as-good RMSE performance of the EM NBE relative to the MAP estimator indicates that Algorithm
2
in the Estimation stage converges to a suitable point estimate across most, if not all, data sets; convergence for a single data set is illustrated in Figure
S6
of the Supplementary Material.

Second, the NBEs represent a trade-off between computational efficiency and statistical efficiency. The speed of the Masking NBE is due to it not requiring likelihood computation or conditional simulation, and because it is not an iterative algorithm. For the spatial Gaussian process model, the MAP estimate is obtained straightforwardly by numerically maximizing the unnormalized posterior density, which is available in closed form. Hence, for this model, the EM NBE provides only a moderate reduction in estimation time compared to the MAP estimator (Table
1
).

These results provide empirical evidence that NBEs can come close to the statistical efficiency of a gold-standard likelihood-based estimator. It will be seen in the next section that NBEs are even more beneficial when the incomplete data likelihood function is unavailable in closed form or is computationally intractable.

3.3
Hidden Potts model

We now consider a hidden Markov random field (e.g.,
Besag,
,
1974
;
Cressie,
,
1993
, Ch. 6;
Rue and Held,
,
2005
) based on the so-called
Potts, (1952)
model.
Consider a regular grid of pixels indexed by
i=1,\dots,n
, where each pixel takes on a label
Y_{i}
from a finite set of discrete states
\mathcal{Q}\equiv\{1,\dots,Q\}
. Then, the Potts model is specified through the conditional distributions,

{\rm{Pr}}(Y_{i}=y\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Y}$}}}_{\backslash i},\beta)\propto\exp\Big\{\beta\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\mathbb{I}(Y_{j}=y)\Big\},\;\text{for }y\in\mathcal{Q},

(20)

where
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Y}$}}}_{\backslash i}
denotes all pixel labels excluding the
i
th pixel;
\beta>0
is a parameter controlling the strength of spatial dependence; and
\mathcal{N}_{i}
contains the indices of the “neighbors” of pixel
i
. Here, we consider a Potts model modified to account for edge effects. Specifically, we let the neighbors
\mathcal{N}_{i}
be the adjacent pixels of pixel
i
, with four neighbors for interior pixels, three for edge pixels, and two for corner pixels. The Potts model exhibits a phase transition at the critical parameter value
\beta_{c}=\log(1+\sqrt{Q})

(
Moores et al.,, 2021
)
, transitioning from disorder when
\beta<\beta_{c}
(where most neighboring pixels do not have the same label) to order when
\beta>\beta_{c}
(most neighboring pixels have the same label).
Its likelihood function, obtained from (
20
), involves a normalizing constant that is computationally intractable for moderate-to-large
n
. In what follows in this section, we focus on a hierarchical extension of the Potts model, the hidden Potts model.

The hidden Potts model extends the Potts model to settings where the labels are not directly observed. The observed data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\equiv(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
are assumed conditionally independent given the latent labels
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Y}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Y}$}}}\equiv(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
, with a label-specific observation distribution:

p(Z_{i}\mid Y_{i}=y,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}_{y});\quad i=1,\dots,n,

(21)

where each label
y\in\mathcal{Q}
is associated with observation-distribution parameters
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}_{y}
that we collect in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}\equiv({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}_{1}^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top},\dots,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}_{Q}^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
. The parameters of the hidden Potts model are
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\equiv(\beta,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
.
As with the standard Potts model, likelihood-based inference in the hidden Potts model is computationally prohibitive except for grids with a small number of grid cells.

The general hidden Potts model given by (
20
) and (
21
) is often made concrete by specifying Gaussian data distributions in (
21
). We focus on this common case in the current simulation study, while in Section
4
, we consider a more complex hidden Potts model in which the observations follow either point-mass or Beta distributions. Specifically,
in the current simulation study,
we assume a hidden Potts model with
Q=3
states and observation distributions

p(Z_{i}\mid Y_{i}=y,{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}_{y})=\text{Gau}(\mu_{y},\sigma_{y}^{2});\quad i=1,\dots,n,

(22)

where each label
y\in\mathcal{Q}
is associated with its own mean
\mu_{y}\in\mathbb{R}
and variance
\sigma^{2}_{y}>0
.
With three hidden states, the phase transition occurs at
\beta_{c}=1.005
.
We adopt the priors
\beta\sim{\text{Unif}}(0,1.5)
,
\mu_{1}\sim\text{{\text{Gau}}}(-1,0.3^{2})
,
\mu_{2}\sim\text{{\text{Gau}}}(0,0.3^{2})
,
\mu_{3}\sim\text{{\text{Gau}}}(1,0.3^{2})
, and
\sigma_{y}\sim{\text{Unif}}(0,1/3)
for
y\in\{1,2,3\}
, and we impose the identifiability constraint
\mu_{1}<\mu_{2}<\mu_{3}
. We estimate the seven unknown parameters
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\equiv(\beta,\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
based on incomplete data on a square grid of size
n=64^{2}=4096
.
To simulate realizations
\textstyle{Y}
,
we use the
Swendsen and Wang, (1987)
algorithm implemented in
bayesImageS

(
Moores et al.,, 2021
)
.
During the Estimation stage of Algorithm
2
, we simulate conditionally on a partially observed, noisy field using
Gibbs sampling (see Section
S6
of the Supplementary Material).

Table
2
and Figure
4
report our results (in Figure
4
we focus on the parameter
\beta
). As with the Gaussian process model, the EM NBE is agnostic to the missingness pattern, and performs well under both missingness models considered in this experiment. By contrast, the Masking NBE is biased for most values of
\beta
when the missingness model is misspecified. The performance of the EM NBE indicates that Algorithm
2
in the Estimation stage converges to a suitable point estimate across most, if not all, data sets; convergence for a single data set is illustrated in Figure
S7
of the Supplementary Material.

Table 2
:

The training time,
estimation time for a single test data set, and empirical RMSE for
two
estimators of the parameters of the hidden Potts model (Section
3.3
).

Estimator

Training time (mins)

Estimation time (s)

RMSE
{}_{\text{MCAR}}

RMSE
{}_{\text{MICB}}

EM NBE

20.4

0.23

0.037

0.042

28.1
