Figure 4
:
Spatial data (first column) simulated from the hidden Potts model of Section
3.3
, where the missingness is of type MCAR (first row) or MICB (second row); empirical distributions (second column) for two estimators of the parameter
\beta
, with
\beta=0.8
fixed (dashed vertical line); and estimates versus true values (third column) for many different values of
\beta
, with the critical parameter value
\beta_{c}=1.005
demarcated by a dashed vertical line.

Finally, this example highlights the advantages of NBEs (and related neural inferential methods) over another popular simulation-based method of inference, approximate Bayesian computation
(
Lintusaari et al.,, 2017
;
Sisson et al.,, 2018
, ABC;)
. ABC typically relies on user-defined summary statistics, which are difficult to construct for latent variable models such as the hidden Potts model. In contrast, NBEs do not require user-defined summary statistics and can be applied directly to high-dimensional data. To provide a comparison in a setting where informative summary statistics are available, in Section
S4
of the Supplementary Material we present an additional simulation study using a spatial version of the generalized hyperbolic distribution. Even in this case where informative summary statistics are available, we find that ABC leads to less statistically efficient estimates than the EM NBE.

4
Application

Here, we consider a remote-sensing application with Arctic sea-ice data. These data are high-dimensional with a missingness mechanism that is difficult to model, but these challenges can be overcome using the EM NBE (Algorithm
2
).

Arctic sea ice plays an important role in regulating our climate: it acts as a reflective surface that reduces the amount of solar energy absorbed by Earth. Melting sea ice exposes darker ocean water, thereby further accelerating the melting process due to an albedo-ice feedback effect. Changes in Arctic sea-ice area and thickness also affect atmospheric circulation and ocean currents, which can influence weather patterns worldwide
(
Cvijanovic et al.,, 2017
)
. Further, Arctic sea ice provides vital habitats for species such as polar bears and seals, and its loss can disrupt fragile ecosystems, thereby affecting biodiversity, food webs, and fisheries
(
Meier et al.,, 2014
)
. Understanding the temporal evolution of Arctic sea ice is therefore crucial for informing policies aimed at mitigating the impacts of climate change, managing resources sustainably, and protecting vulnerable ecosystems
(
United Nations,, 2024
)
.

In this application, we consider spatial data of Arctic sea-ice proportion, that is, the proportion of sea ice in grid cells at given spatial locations (also commonly referred to as sea-ice concentration), produced by the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) as part of their National Snow and Ice Data Center’s (NSIDC) Climate Data Record
(
Meier et al.,, 2021
, CRD;)
. The data are derived from passive microwave remote sensing retrievals from the Nimbus 7 satellite and the F8, F11, F13, and F17 satellites of the Defence Meteorological Satellite Program, projected onto 25km
\times
25km grid cells within a region of the Northern Hemisphere spanning longitudes 180°E to 180°W and latitudes at or above 60
∘
N
(
Zhang and Cressie,, 2020
;
Meier et al.,, 2021
)
. Arctic-sea-ice cover typically reaches its annual minimum in September
(
Parkinson,, 2014
)
, and we therefore base our analysis on the ice cover on the first day of September in each year.
Our data set comprises 45 spatial images (one image for each year between 1979 and 2023), with each image containing
199\times 219=43581
grid cells, and we analyze each year separately.

Figure
5
shows that the data are incomplete and that the missingness patterns are relatively complicated. Here, missingness occurs for several reasons, including cloud cover and unpredictable issues with the remote-sensing instrument
(
Meier et al.,, 2021
)
. The data are also subject to a more consistent form of missingness around the North Pole: this area, called the Arctic Pole Hole, changes in size over time as it is a function of both the remote-sensing instrument and the prevailing atmospheric conditions
(
Meier et al.,, 2021
)
.

Figure 5
:
Arctic sea-ice data from the first day of September for the years 1979, 1993, 1995, and 2023. Faint gray lines denote coastlines, with Greenland appearing at the bottom. The data are subject to both random sources of missingness (e.g., cloud cover) and more systematic sources of missingness due to remote-sensing limitations (e.g., the Arctic Pole Hole).

Previous studies of Artic sea-ice
(
Parkinson,, 2014
;
Zhang and Cressie,, 2020
, e.g.,)
often apply a fixed threshold (e.g., 15%) to classify each grid cell as either “ice” or “not ice”. This binarization simplifies modeling but it has important limitations: the threshold is arbitrary, it discards valuable information from the underlying data (which gives proportions of sea-ice cover), and it can bias estimates of sea-ice area (the total ice-covered region). Instead, we model the sea-ice proportions directly using a hidden Potts model (see (
20
)) with
Q=4
states, spatial-dependence parameter
\beta>0
, and the following observation distributions:

\displaystyle p(Z_{i}\mid Y_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=\delta_{0}(Z_{i}),\qquad

\displaystyle p(Z_{i}\mid Y_{i}=2)=\text{Beta}(Z_{i};a_{1},b_{1}),

\displaystyle p(Z_{i}\mid Y_{i}=3)

\displaystyle=\text{Beta}(Z_{i};a_{2},b_{2}),\qquad

\displaystyle p(Z_{i}\mid Y_{i}=4)=\delta_{1}(Z_{i}),

where
\delta_{x}(\cdot)
denotes the Dirac delta function that models point mass at
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\text{Beta}(z;a,b)
,
z\in(0,1)
, denotes the Beta density function with shape parameters
a>0
and
b>0
, and for identifiability we impose ordering of the means:
a_{1}(a_{1}+b_{1})^{-1}<a_{2}(a_{2}+b_{2})^{-1}
. This hidden Potts model captures the multimodal structure evident in the empirical histogram of sea-ice proportions (see Figure
S9
of the Supplementary Material). The inclusion of two Beta components allows flexible modeling of the continuous observations in
(0,1)
, which exhibit strong spatial dependence (see, e.g., Figure
S12
of the Supplementary Material). Conditional simulation, which is required by our EM NBE (Algorithm
2
) and for prediction at grid cells with missing pixels, can be done using MCMC, as described in Section
S6
of the Supplementary Material.

In our hidden Potts model, the unknown parameters are
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\equiv(\beta,a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
. We use a relatively uninformative prior for the spatial-dependence parameter
\beta>0
, namely,
\beta\sim{\text{Unif}}(0,1.5)
. For the Beta shape parameters, we adopt the informative priors

a_{1}\sim{\text{Unif}}(2,5)
,

b_{1}\sim{\text{Unif}}(2,5)
,

a_{2}\sim{\text{Unif}}(2,5)
,
and
b_{2}\sim{\text{Unif}}(0,1)
.
Given the large data size, the computationally intractable model, and the complicated missingness mechanisms, our EM NBE (Algorithm
2
) is well suited for inference in this application. We trained the NBE using the same settings given in Sections
3.1
and
3.3
, with a total training time (including data simulation) of 32 minutes. We then applied the EM NBE to each of the 45 images. The total time for estimating the 45 parameter vectors
\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}t=1,\dots,45\}
, for each of the 45 years, was 44 seconds.

Figure
6
, left panel, shows estimates
\{\hat{\beta}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}t=1,\dots,45\}
, as well as 95% pointwise confidence intervals (Figure
S10
of the Supplementary Material presents the corresponding plots for all parameters). To obtain these intervals, we used a separate parametric bootstrap for each year. Specifically, for each year, we simulated 100 data sets from the fitted model, removed data from the same grid cells that were missing in the observed data set, and then applied the EM NBE again to each of the 100 simulated (incomplete) data sets. All estimates of
\beta
are larger than the critical value
\beta_{c}=\log(1+\sqrt{Q})=1.099
, confirming a strong tendency for neighboring grid cells to share the same label.

Figure 6
:
Analysis of Arctic sea-ice data. (Left) EM NBE estimates of the spatial dependence parameter
\beta
in (
20
) versus year. Shaded areas display 95% pointwise confidence intervals obtained using parametric bootstrap sampling, while the dashed horizontal line indicates the critical value
\beta_{c}=\log(1+\sqrt{Q})=1.099
. (Center-left) Predictions of sea-ice area versus year. Note that 95% prediction intervals are plotted, but these are not visible due to the low uncertainty in the predictions. (Center-right) Arctic sea-ice data for September 1, 1995. Faint gray lines denote coastlines, with Greenland appearing at the bottom. (Right) Predicted sea-ice proportion for all grid cells within the gray box of the center-right panel.

Figure
6
, center-left panel, shows predictions of sea-ice area as a function of year. To make these predictions, we imputed missing values by simulating 100 times from the fitted model (of the corresponding year) conditionally on the observed data, and we treated the mean of these conditional simulations as the prediction. In line with the general scientific consensus, our analysis indicates that sea-ice area is decreasing dramatically over time, nearly halving over the study period. The center-right and right panels of Figure
6
show the incomplete data from September 1, 1995, and the resulting predictions of sea-ice proportion in a box containing a subset of the incomplete data (see Figures
S11
and
S12
of the Supplementary Material for individual conditional simulations and predictions of both the data and the hidden labels). Due to the strong spatial dependence, the predicted proportions in the interior of the ice sheet and in regions far from ice are close to 1 and 0, respectively; however, there is greater variability within the region corresponding to the sea-ice boundary. Post-training, inference (including bootstrap) for all 45 years of data took a total of 1.7 hours clock time. This can be compared to
Zhang and Cressie,
’s (
2020
) fully Bayesian inference who, with a binary model and with a smaller data set, needed more than a day of clock time on a comparable high-performance computer.

Finally, we note evidence of spatial nonstationarity in these Arctic sea-ice data: see Figure
S13
of the Supplementary Material, which summarizes our analysis over a subdomain of interest, namely, the Canadian Arctic Archipelago. Future work in this application might hence consider spatially-varying versions of
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
b_{1}
, and
b_{2}
. The advantage of the proposed EM NBE is that more complicated (nonstationary) models will not alter the workflow, and that more complicated models can be fitted and assessed with relative ease.

5
Conclusion

Incomplete data are ubiquitous in applications of AI, arising from various sources such as cloud cover, equipment malfunctions, and data corruption. In this article, we focus on the problem of missing data in the context of neural Bayes estimation, which uses neural networks to map data to point estimates of parameters. We first discuss and give new insights on the masking approach of
Wang et al., (2024)
(Algorithm
1
), where inference is performed on an extended data set containing the observed data and auxiliary variables that encode the missingness pattern. The masking approach has advantages: It can be used to quickly generate Bayes estimates under general loss functions; it only requires marginal (i.e., unconditional) simulation from the data model; and it is theoretically well motivated (see Theorems
1
and
2
in Section
S1
of the Supplementary Material). However, its major disadvantage is that it requires a stochastic model for the missingness mechanism, which can lead to bias and statistical inefficiency when the model is wrong and the data are high-dimensional. We therefore propose an alternative approach that is based on the MCEM algorithm, where the E- and M-steps are approximated using conditional simulation and an NBE that returns the (approximate) MAP estimate from the conditionally-simulated complete data. Our EM approach (Algorithm
2
) is likelihood-free, in the sense that it does not require evaluation or knowledge of the likelihood function; it is fast, since it does not require numerical optimization at each iteration; and, in contrast to the masking approach, it is agnostic to the missingness pattern of the observed data. Moreover, our research can be viewed as a prototype problem that indicates how improvements could be made in AI by introducing statistical inferential tools.

Our EM approach to neural Bayes estimation with incomplete data relies
on conditional simulation, which can be a computational bottleneck for certain models. Future research will explore the use of approximate conditional simulation
(
Wu et al.,, 2023
;
Simkus et al.,, 2023
;
Simkus and Gutmann,, 2025
, e.g.,)
to extend the applicability of our EM approach to models for which conditional simulation is intractable or computationally prohibitive. While we focus on point estimation, our insights on the masking approach extend to methods that use it to approximate the full posterior distribution of the parameters and more general inference frameworks
(
Gloeckler et al.,, 2024
, e.g.,)
. In these contexts, the data-augmentation algorithm of
Tanner and Wong, (1987)
offers advantages analogous to those of our EM approach in the point-estimation setting. Finally, neural Bayes estimation requires specifying a potentially large set of hyperparameters related to the neural network’s architecture and its training. Although automated search methods

(
Elsken et al.,, 2019
, see, e.g.,)

can be useful,
and
Rödder et al., (2025)
provide important initial theoretical results, further developments are needed to establish practical guidelines for hyperparameter selection.

RH was also partially supported by KAUST Office of Sponsored Research (OSR) under Award No. OSR-CRG2020-4394. Supplementary Material for “Neural Parameter Estimation with Incomplete Data”

[1]Matthew Sainsbury-Dale[2,3]Andrew Zammit-Mangion[3]Noel Cressie[1]Raphaël Huser

Supplementary Material for “Neural Parameter Estimation with Incomplete Data”

In Section
S1
, we provide a theoretical rationale for the masking approach of
Wang et al., (2024)
. In Section
S2
, we discuss the asymptotic behavior of the Monte Carlo EM (MCEM) algorithm when the conventional update is approximate. In Section
S3
, we consider several continuous approximations of the 0–1 loss function that may be used when training an NBE to approximate the MAP estimator.
In Section
S4
, we conduct an additional simulation experiment using a spatial version of the generalized hyperbolic (GH) distribution.
In Section
S5
, we propose and illustrate the benefits of using an ensemble of deep neural networks in the context of neural Bayes estimation.
In Section
S6
, we discuss Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling in hidden Potts models. Finally, in Section
S7
, we provide additional figures supporting our methodology.

S1
Rationale for the masking approach

Here, we use a sufficiency argument to show that the masking approach of
Wang et al., (2024)
does not lead to any loss of information on
\textstyle{\theta}
.

Theorem 1
.

Let the complete data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be distributed according to a family of probability distributions indexed by
\textstyle{\theta}
. Partition
\textstyle{Z}
into the components
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}
containing the observed and missing elements, respectively, where it is assumed that there is at least one observation. Define the ordered set
\mathcal{I}_{1}\equiv\{i\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z_{i}\text{ is observed}\}
such that
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}=(Z_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in\mathcal{I}_{1})^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\top}
, and let
\textstyle{U}
and
\textstyle{W}
be defined as in Equations (
4
) and (
5
) of the main text. Then,

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{T}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})\equiv({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}),

is a sufficient statistic for
\textstyle{\theta}
.

Proof.

Given that there is at least one observation,
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is non-empty and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
is well defined. Now,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
represent all of the available information from which to make inference on
\textstyle{\theta}
, that is, together they are sufficient for
\textstyle{\theta}
, and hence we need only show that
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{T}$}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
defines a one-to-one mapping from the space of
({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})
to that of
({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}})
, since any one-to-one transformation of a sufficient statistic is itself sufficient
(
Casella and Berger,, 2001
, pg. 280)
.
First, note that the construction of
\textstyle{U}
using Equation (
4
) of the main text can be equivalently written as
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\odot{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}+c({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{1}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}})
, where
\odot
denotes elementwise multiplication, and
\textstyle{1}
denotes the vector of 1s of appropriate dimension. Now, for any
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{a}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{a}
, any
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{b}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{b}
, and any
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{T}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{a},\mathcal{I}_{1}^{a})={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{T}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{b},\mathcal{I}_{1}^{b})

\displaystyle\implies({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}^{a},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a})=({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}^{b},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{b})

\displaystyle\implies{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}^{a}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}^{b}\text{ and }{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{b}\neq{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{0}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{0}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{0}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{0}$}}}

\displaystyle\implies{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{a}\odot{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a}+c({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{1}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a})={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{b}\odot{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a}+c({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{1}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{1}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a})\text{ and }\mathcal{I}_{1}^{a}=\mathcal{I}_{1}^{b}

\displaystyle\implies{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{a}\odot{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}^{b}\odot{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a}\text{ and }\mathcal{I}_{1}^{a}=\mathcal{I}_{1}^{b}

\displaystyle\implies{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{a}={\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}^{b}\text{ and }\mathcal{I}_{1}^{a}=\mathcal{I}_{1}^{b},

since
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}^{a}\neq{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{0}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{0}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{0}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{0}$}}}
. Therefore,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{T}$}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
defines a one-to-one mapping from the space of
({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})
to that of
({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}})
and, hence,
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{T}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{T}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})
is sufficient for
\textstyle{\theta}
.
∎

Theorem
1
is important in establishing that the masking approach makes full use of the information contained in the data. However, using
\textstyle{U}
and
\textstyle{W}
in place of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
in a neural Bayes estimation setting requires assigning a distribution to
\mathcal{I}_{1}
(or equivalently, to
\textstyle{W}
). Using an argument that closely follows that of
Richards et al., (2024, Theorem 1)
and
Sainsbury-Dale et al., (2025, Theorem 1)
, we now show that, under mild conditions, the Bayes estimator is invariant to this distribution.

Theorem 2
.

Let the complete data
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be distributed according to a family of probability distributions indexed by
\textstyle{\theta}
. Partition
\textstyle{Z}
into the components
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}
containing the observed and missing elements, respectively. Let
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\in\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}=\{0,1\}^{n}
be defined as in Equations (
4
) and (
5
) of the main text. Let
L\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Theta\times\Theta\to[0,\infty)
denote a loss function, and assume that the Bayes estimate
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}
under this loss function exists, is unique, and has finite posterior expected loss
for all
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\in\mathcal{U}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}
. Then the Bayes estimator
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}})
that returns
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}
for any
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\in\mathcal{U}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}
is invariant to the distribution
p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}(\cdot)
of
\textstyle{W}
, provided

(i)

p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})>0
for all
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}
,

(ii)

\textstyle{W}
and
\textstyle{\theta}
are independent.

Proof.

For all fixed
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\in\mathcal{U}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}
, the Bayes estimate
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}
minimizes the posterior expected loss; that is,

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}=\argmin_{\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}}\int_{\Theta}L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}})p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}})\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}.

(S1)

Consider now the Bayes estimator
\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}})
that returns the Bayes estimate for any fixed
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\in\mathcal{U}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}

(see
Brown and Purves,, 1973
, for a proof of the existence of a Borel measurable Bayes estimator under mild conditions)
. Since the posterior expected loss is, by assumption, bounded and nonnegative for all
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\in\mathcal{U}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}
, we have that

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}(\cdot,\cdot)=\argmin_{\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}}\tilde{p}_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})\int_{\mathcal{U}}\int_{\Theta}L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}))p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\textrm{d}F({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}),

(S2)

for any strictly positive conditional (on
\textstyle{W}
) probability measure
F({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})
and any strictly positive probability mass function
\tilde{p}_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}(\cdot)
on
\mathcal{W}
. Choosing
\textrm{d}F({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})=p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}
for the conditional measure in (
S2
), we have that

Applying Bayes rule to
p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})
yields

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}(\cdot,\cdot)=\argmin_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}}\int_{\mathcal{U}}\int_{\Theta}L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}))\frac{p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})}{p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})}\tilde{p}_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\textrm{d}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}.

(S3)

From (
S3
) we see that if
p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})=p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}})
, then

\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}^{\star}(\cdot,\cdot)=\argmin_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}\in\mathcal{W}}\int_{\mathcal{U}}\int_{\Theta}L({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}},\hat{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{w}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{w}$}}}))p_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{u}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{u}$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\ for any positive
\tilde{p}_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{W}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{W}$}}}}(\cdot)
on
\mathcal{W}
, thus completing the proof.
∎

Theorem
2
establishes that, apart from the requirement of strict positivity, the choice of distribution for
\mathcal{I}_{1}
(equivalently,
\textstyle{W}
) is immaterial for the Bayes estimator. However, in practice, NBEs are trained on a finite number of realizations from the statistical model, and the empirical Bayes risk is therefore affected by Monte Carlo error that does depend on this distribution, particularly in high-dimensional settings where the number of possible missingness patterns (
2^{n}
) is very large. This pitfall is demonstrated in Section
3
of the main text, where we show that choosing a distribution for
\mathcal{I}_{1}
that assigns low probability to the observed missingness pattern can yield a
statistically inefficient
estimator. For further discussion, see Section
2.2
of the main text.

S2
Asymptotic behavior of approximate MCEM sequences

Chan and Ledolter, (1995)
showed formally that, for large Monte Carlo sample size
m
, a sequence generated by the MCEM algorithm behaves approximately as a first-order autoregressive (AR(1)) process centered on the conventional EM update. They also showed that, in the vicinity of an isolated local maximizer
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{*}
of the incomplete-data posterior density, a sequence generated by the MCEM algorithm behaves approximately as a stationary AR(1) process with mean
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{*}
. Here, we extend these results to the more general setting where each MCEM update is only approximate, for example, when each update is performed using an NBE, like in our proposed Algorithm
2
of the main text.

We first recall the EM and MCEM algorithms that were reviewed in Section
2.3.1
of the main text, and the family of approximate MCEM algorithms introduced in Section
2.3.2
of the main text. There we use
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}
to denote the subvectors of
\textstyle{Z}
that are treated as observed and missing, respectively, and we use
\textstyle{Z}
to denote the complete data. In what follows, we take a Bayesian perspective, based on maximization of the posterior density; the frequentist version of the subsequent algorithms are recovered by taking the prior, which can be viewed as a penalty function, to be
\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\propto 1
. The expected complete-data log-posterior, central to EM-based algorithms, is given by

Q(
\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}
’ ∣
\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}
) = log
π
(
\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}
’) +
E
_
\scriptscriptstyle{Z}
_2 ∣
\scriptscriptstyle{Z}
_1,
\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}
logp(
\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}
’ ∣
\scriptscriptstyle{Z}
_1,
\scriptscriptstyle{Z}
_2)

where the ‘prime’ notation
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}
is used to denote an alternative parameter vector (and not the transpose of
\textstyle{\theta}
). The EM algorithm
(
Dempster et al.,, 1977
;
Wu,, 1983
;
McLachlan and Krishnan,, 2008
)
iteratively finds
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(l)}
such that

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(l)}=M({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(l-1)})\equiv\argmax_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\in\Theta}Q({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{(l-1)});\quad l=1,2,\dots,

(S4)

where
M(\cdot)
is deterministic and
\Theta
denotes the parameter space. In the MCEM algorithm
(
Wei and Tanner,, 1990
)
, one replaces
Q({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
with a Monte Carlo approximation,

Q_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})=\log\pi({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}^{(j)}),

(S5)

where
m
is the Monte Carlo sample size and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}^{(j)}\sim p({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{2}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{Z}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{Z}$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, independently for

.
Hence, the MCEM algorithm iteratively finds
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}_{m}^{(l)}
such that

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}_{m}^{(l)}=M_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}_{m}^{(l-1)})\equiv\argmax_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\in\Theta}Q_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}_{m}^{(l-1)});\quad l=1,2,\dots.

In this work, we consider an approximate version of the MCEM algorithm that we write as:

\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}_{m}^{(l)}=\tilde{M}_{m}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}_{m}^{(l-1)})\equiv M_{m}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}_{m}^{(l-1)})+{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\delta}$}}}_{m}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}_{m}^{(l-1)});\quad l=1,2,\dots,

(S6)

where
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\delta}$}}}_{m}(\cdot)

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\delta}$}}}_{m}(\cdot)
is due to our use of an NBE to approximate the random map

where

(S11)

To derive the asymptotic properties of the innovations defined in (
S11
), we first derive expressions for the asymptotic expectation and asymptotic variance of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\xi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\xi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\xi}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\xi}$}}}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
, defined in (
S9
).
To proceed, we consider a second expansion of the gradient of (
S5
), this time around
M_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})
. Neglecting higher-order terms,

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}}Q_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}^{\prime}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})

\displaystyle\approx\nabla_{\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}}Q_{m}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\big|_{\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}=M_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})}+\nabla^{2}_{\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}}Q_{m}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})\big|_{\tilde{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}}}=M_{m}({\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\theta}$}}})}\left\{{\mathchoice{\hbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\theta}$}}{\hbox{\boldmat
